Hi. I’m Evan Pagan. Welcome to the Guru Master Class Content Summit. In this program, we’re going to learn
to create valuable information products that help customers and grow our businesses.
What is an information product, and why are they important? Information products are anything that takes
information, knowledge, wisdom, and encapsulates it in a form that another human can take, learn from, and
use. Ideally, it’s very similar to sitting with you in a room and actually learning from you directly. Those are the
most valuable information products.
In my hands, I’m holding two books. You may have seen these books before. This book right here, this is a book
called How to Read a Book by Mortimer Adler. This book sells for $16.00 in the bookstore, $16.00. This is an
amazing book. Everyone should read this book. This is a lifetime of wisdom about how to learn encapsulated
in a package that costs $16.00 that you can read in a few hours of your time.
This book is a book called Stealth Marketing by Jay Abraham. This is also a very, very important book. This
is a book that everyone should read. This book, when Jay sold it new, was $1,000.00, $1,000.00 for this book.
What I’ve learned from this book has made me a lot of money. Bargain, in the big scheme, maybe even more
of a bargain in terms of ROI than this book.
But they’re both books, right? They’re both just paper. How is it that this guy sells this book for $16.00, and this

Your business is very similar to those books, metaphorically speaking. Just like they’re both made out of paper

Something you may not know, or you may not have realized, is that we have been transitioning, especially over
the last just several decades, into a knowledge-based society. Okay, we’re actually transitioning to a knowledgebased society and world.
And what I mean by that is, a couple of hundred years ago, what most people did, and what most people were
concerned with, and most people were doing through their day, was planting the fields, harvesting, making
food, building houses, blacksmithing – you follow – making clothing. That’s what most people did. They did
the things they needed to do to survive, to put a roof over their head, and food in their mouth.
Well, nowadays, we can produce so much more food, so many more houses, so much more in the way of
clothing with automation and powerful business practices. And because we’ve gotten so good at extracting
things from the planet, which may or may not be the best thing in the long-run – but that conversation aside,
we’ve gotten so good at automating all those things and outsourcing them and becoming efficient that it’s freed
up our time.
We can grow – I don’t know – something like 30 or 50 times as much food on a piece of land now than we were
able to grow just a couple of years ago. Modern techniques have made subsistence in the first world, where we
live and where we’re filming this program. They’ve made it a given. We expect it.

Mindset No. 1: Humans are irrational. Humans are irrational. Now, we humans like to think that we’re very
rational. In fact, we’ve got a bunch of mechanisms in our head that allow us to rationalize just about anything
we do and make it make sense, right? So we’ve got this mechanism that allows us to explain everything and
make it make sense to us in a certain way.
And watch it happen. Someone will be saying something that’s totally crazy, that’s not serving them at all, and
you can say, “I’m not trying to tell you what to do or anything or criticize you, but it really seems like what you’re
doing right now is counter-productive. You’re actually moving the opposite direction from where you want to
go in life.” And they go, “Yeah, but he’s a jerk,” as if that is a good reason to do the thing that gets you far from
what you want.
So humans are irrational. Essentially, every thought we have, every feeling we have, and every action we take
has some type of irrational motive underneath it. The sooner you can wake up to that reality about yourself,
the sooner you can wake up to the reality about others.

And then when you have it explained to you, “Okay, so what we do is we project onto other people; we don’t look

We still do it, even after we’re told about it. Even after humans are told about their biases, we still have them.

So humans are irrational. We’re driven by our passions. We’re essentially machine-like in everything that we

machine-like and irrational, and everything that you’re thinking, feeling, and doing is driven by these irrational

Why is this valuable to know? Because what most authors, speakers, coaches, people who are trained to create
information products and help other people, what they’re doing is they’re trying to appeal to the rational mind
of the other humans that they’re talking to. They’re talking to the logical, thinking brain.
Jared Diamond, the guy that wrote Guns, Germs, & Steel, he wrote another book that most people haven’t
heard of but what might be even more important for humans to have read.
And that book is called The Third Chimpanzee, The Third Chimpanzee. Most people don’t even know that
there are two species of chimp, and there are. There’s the regular standard chimp, and then there’s what they
call the pygmy chimp or the bonobo, and they’re very different from each other. Well, what Jared Diamond
says is that we are the third chimpanzee and that, in fact, the chimp isn’t our closest relative; we are the chimp’s
closest relative.
And Eugene Schwartz, the great copywriter, who wrote Breakthrough Advertising – I read one of his speeches,
and it said, “You’ve got to remember that your prospects, you’re not talking to the logical human brain. You’re
talking to the chimpanzee brain.”
That’s what you’re dealing with here. And I don’t mean to demean or belittle people or get into some argument
about evolution or anything. That’s not where I’m trying to go. I’m just trying to say that, if you use the mindset
that humans are irrational, and you’re talking to the chimpanzee brain, you’re going to get a lot further.
Mindset No. 2: Your product is your customer’s obstacle. Your product is your customer’s obstacle. In other
words, your customer is where they are, and they want to get to a particular place in their life. They want to
accomplish a result. They want to get an outcome. They want to get a benefit.
You might say, “I have the perfect thing for that. Buy my information product for $997.00, and you’ll get the
outcome you want. Aren’t I special?” What your prospect is thinking in the chimp brain is, “That stands
between me and the outcome that I want. It’s the obstacle that I have to get around. It’s the hassle. It’s the
thing that costs me money.”

Mindset No. 3: Be a marketer, not an expert. Be a marketer, not an expert. When I teach, when I speak at

They leave having bought the car but having not liked the experience, just wanted to get out of there. They

Mindset No. 4: This is an equation. Product value equals believable result. Now, I’ve summed this up and
generalized it. I’m going to explain what it means in a second. But product value equals believable result.
In this case, I’m talking about a customer that doesn’t really know you that well and is trying to make the call,

Believable result, that’s where it’s at, the outcome that they’re going to get. It’s not what they’re going to learn or

to get? What’s the outcome at the end of the day?” Product value equals believable result, believable result. So
what’s a result that you can product for your customer that’s believable that they will buy?

So to them, the extra weight on their body that’s making them feel bad, that’s the way it works. It’s really like
that to them. It’s as real as the pen or pencil that you’re holding in their hand or the seat you’re sitting on or the
floor you’re standing on. It’s that real to them.
And you’re not going to talk them out of it in your marketing and in the beginning. If you get them into a room,
and you get them into an intensive process, you might be able to wake them up to that for a second, but you’re
not going to do it in your marketing. You’re not going to do it before they know you.
That’s the reality that they live in. They have an unmet emotional need. Okay, they’re having a negative
emotion, or they’re having a lack of positive emotion. And they’re blaming it on something outside. They’re
saying, “If only I could get this thing, then I would be happy. If only that person would stop complaining, then
I wouldn’t feel bad.”
So this is where we want to start, when we’re creating valuable information products and content. We want to
ask ourselves, “What’s the unmet emotional need? What’s the emotional hot button? What’s that driver that’s
inside of my customer that’s driving them to try to figure this thing out, to look for solutions?”
And then we want to ask, “What’s the thing they’re blaming it on? What’s the external thing in their mind
that’s responsible for all of this?” I said before, “Humans are irrational.” I’m always asking myself the question,

Because in their mind, they’re making up a movie. They’ve got a fantasy that they’re going through in their

can go jump into that world with them and say, “Hey, I’m in here with you. And we get it, and everybody else
doesn’t.”
And if I’m really good at empathizing and being compassionate, I can really get into that reality, and I can relate

a situation in my life where I had something almost exactly the same. There’s probably someone in my life

Strategy No. 2: Narrow your focus. Narrow your focus. In western culture, for some reason, we full a pull to
widen our focus. So if we’re going to be teaching in the health and fitness area, and we’re going to be teaching
weight loss, and I say, “Great. What do you want to write your book about?” the person who’s an expert in
weight loss will say, “I want to teach total health.”
And I’ll say, “Well, maybe weight loss, isn’t that your specialty? Why don’t we drill down into weight loss and
find a niche market into that that’s untapped, right, where there are a bunch of people running around that
have needs, and they’re not getting them met?” And they say, “Well, yeah, but I don’t just want to teach weight
loss. I want to be everything to everyone. Remember Jesus? When I read the Bible, I relate to him. That’s what
I want to do.” All right, we zoom out.
Narrow your focus. When you narrow your focus, it forces you to be specific, forces you to think about one
other human being and their needs. And that’s also a lot more powerful when it comes to marketing and
creating categories.
Strategy No. 3: Think external, specific, measurable, tangible, and verifiable. Think external, specific,
measurable, tangible, verifiable. Think external, specific, measurable, tangible, verifiable. What is implied by
this is avoid thinking internal, conceptual, theoretical, idealistic, all that kind of stuff.
Now, we want to think about the fantasies and the emotions that people have going on. But when it’s time to
create the product itself, when it’s time to act, we want to be thinking about external, concrete, measurable
things.
We don’t’ want to say to our prospect, “If you read this book, you’re going to get to your ideal weight,” because
that’s not external and specific and measurable and verifiable and concreter, and I can’t verify it and get a group
of people together and say, “Did that happen?”
What we want to say is, “If you read this book, you will lose 17 pounds in 90 days, and I guarantee it.” That’s it
because losing 17 pounds in 90 days is very specific and tangible and concrete and measurable and verifiable
and all that stuff.
I’ve noticed one of the biggest challenges I have when I’m coaching people, in choosing a niche, creating a
product, creating a piece of content, writing a sales letter, especially teachers because I think they tend to be a
little bit more conceptual – they can see the bigger picture. They zoom out and zoom inside, in a way, and talk
about all the theoretical reasons why someone would want to learn this and all the try stuff and talk about the
feelings.
That’s not what we want to do. When we’re creating a product, we want to challenge ourselves to be external,
specific, measurable, tangible. Now, remember, it starts with the internal, irrational, emotional driver, that
fantasy that’s in the prospect. You want to understand that. Remember, they’re blaming something outside

Strategy No. 4: This has to do with your voice, your personality. Be what I call edgy-authentic, edgy-authentic.
It’s my experience that people like to learn from what you might think of as an idealized version of themselves,
an idealized version of themselves.
They don’t want to learn from someone that’s too different from them. They don’t want to learn from someone

to learn from someone that’s maybe 25 percent more evolved than them, okay, kind of an idealized version of
them, what they could really see as possible.
And when you are using this voice that I call edgy-authentic, what you do is you get real about things. You talk
about – you’re a little more honest than they would be, a little bit more honest about the reality of life, a little
bit more honest about how you do things, share a little more than they would, a little more honest about the
problems that you have in life, a little bit more honest about the way things really are. You’re just a little bit
more radically honest. But you’re also a little bit more edgy, a little bit more edgy.

Now, this is going to sound like a complete contradiction to everything I just said about how they’re irrational,

And you say, “Okay, what am I going to have to do to get them to take responsibility? Well, first I’m going to

because we all have a huge vested interest in blaming everything outside ourselves for our negative emotions.
And those negative emotions are what drive most purchases of information products, in way or another, in my
experience. And so because we have that huge vested interest, we don’t want someone to take our excuse away.
So you need to become very skilled. You need to become a professional, if you want to help other people take

But when you take responsibility for getting the other person to take responsibility, it actually forces you to
be better at coming up with the magic bullets and narrowing your niche and your focus, communicating with
power words, being edgy-authentic because now you need to pull a lot of people in.
You need to build a lot of report and a lot of relationship with them, so they will trust you enough so that, when

All right, you’re good. I’m going to take it away again for another second” – you following me on this one? Take
responsibility for getting your customer to take responsibility.
So it’s super counter-intuitive for most teachers, authors, speakers, teachers, all those types, super counterintuitive. What we want to do is we want to stand there, and we want to babble and tell a bunch of stuff. And

you can understand it from a lot of perspectives, and you can triangulate what’s going on. But we’re starting
with what’s important to the customer.
In my estimate, children hear about 1 percent of what parents tell them to do, even if they comply. And in my
experience, children hear about 100 percent of what’s important to them. In my experience, customers hear
about 1 percent of what you tell them, “You should do this. This is the right way to do it.” They hear 1 percent,
maybe, and they hear 100 percent of what’s important to them.
Why is this? The mechanism I talked about earlier that, when you know where you’re going and what you want,
the reticular activating system, it fires up, and it starts seeing all of the pieces that will help get you there. Well,
the reticular activating system is that part of the brain that’s always on the lookout for something that might be
important. It’s looking for danger. It’s looking for opportunity.
And once you get that part of your brain programmed, whether you do it consciously or unconsciously, like
most people do, it’s going to do its job. It’s going to look for what’s important to that human.

Most authors, most teachers typically start with what they think is important for the other person to learn,
“Here’s what you need to know.” What customers want you to start with is what they think is important, what
they think the problem is. They want you to start with the idealistic reality that they’re living in. That’s what
they want.
One of my teachers from several years ago, a brilliant guy, he used to say, “People don’t go to therapists because
they want to get fixed. They go to therapists because they want someone to validate their problems.” They play
a game with the therapist called, “I’m going to tell you how bad it is so that you can say to me, ‘Oh, yeah, I’d feel
the same way if I were you. You’re valid that you feel that way. Yes, it really is this other problem, and let’s go to
work on fixing it.’” That’s why we go. We think we want to get out of it, but we don’t really.
So what I like to do is start with the fears and frustrations and the wants and aspirations of the customer, try to
figure out what the result is that they want and just talk about that and link everything to that.
And if you’ve got a customer who wants to lose 19 pounds in 90 days because they want to fit into their wedding
dress, that’s it. Everything that you say, do, sell has got to link back up to 19 pounds in 90 days. Everything
you’re going to talk about, all of it, has to link back up to that, has to be a straight line to it in some way. And
whatever you think they need to do, whatever the right answer is, whatever’s the real deal, is all irrelevant if it
doesn’t connect up to those things that that person wants.
Fortunately, you can connect up what people really need to the result that they want, and you can do it in a
skillful way. But you’ve got to remember the only thing that they care about is their needs, their irrational
fantasy, that external thing that they’re trying to achieve that they think is going to be the answer to all their
problems.
Got to start with what’s important to the customer. If you want to make real money selling information, if
you want to become financially independent, if you want a business that runs itself and pays you while you’re

One of the most important things that I’ve ever learned in my life was an insight that I got from Wyatt that
I have turned into an equation. And the simple equation goes like this: Learning equals behavior change.
Learning equals behavior change.
Now, this is a generalization, but it’s a very important ah-ha to get. It’s very important to get that learning isn’t,
“Oh, I can repeat it.” Learning isn’t, “Oh, I think I get it now.” Learning isn’t remembering something. It’s not
conceptual. Learning is behavior change. When a person’s behavior changes, that’s when they learn.

So let’s talk about a simplified version of levels of learning. I’m going to give you three. Okay, I’m going to give
you three levels of learning. And you might think of these as building upon each other because they really
do.
But the first level of learning we might call, “Insight.” Okay, the first level of learning we might call, “Insight.”
And an insight is when we say, “Oh, now I get it. Oh, now I understand. Oh, now I can repeat it back to you.
Oh, I think I see how that works.” It’s kind of the informational level, where you understand it in your head.
You get it intellectually.
The second level of learning we’re going to call, “Action,” when you are taking action. That’s the second level
of learning. First level of learning is insight. But again, we just described learning as behavior change. The
second level is action. That’s where we really start to learn. That’s when it becomes real in our life. That’s
when we start getting feedback from the world, and we start acting. Things work. They don’t work. We try it
a different way, right? We start refining. We get better and better and better.
Only have we take action does our human learning modeling behavior system really kick in, and we can start
modifying what we’re doing, and we start to get it in our physiology and our emotions, in our intellect.
So the second level would be action, and then the third level is what we’re going to call, “Transformation.” And
this is when a human being changes their self-image, when they see themselves differently. And when they
change their self-image, when they evolve to the next level, many behaviors change, and many insights start
happening on their own.
All right, so the levels affect each other. Level 1: Insight. Level 2: Action. Level 3: Transformation.
So how do we create each of these three levels? How do you create insight, or knowing? How do you create
action, which delivers experience? And how do you create transformation, a change in self-image? How do
you do these things?
Well, to create insight, or to create knowing, another lesson that I got from Wyatt is that humans really don’t
like to learn new things, and they’re not very good at it. New is very challenging. People like to think they’re
learning new things. They like to have information presented as new and as news. And they like it to be
perceived that way, but they don’t really like to learn actual new things.
And the proof I have for this is think about the news, which is just new plural, news. That’s what it is, a
pluralized version of new. What’s in the news most of the time, the general news? Is there really anything new
in there?

Not really. You follow me? It’s not really anything new. It’s really the same old stuff that’s been happening
forever. Sometimes it’s frustrating to talk to me and try to tell me about the latest news. Someone says, “Wow,
did you hear about that plane crash? 300 people died.” I’m like, “Yeah, that happens every couple of years. It’s
part of being human, right? Planes crash. It’s not really that” – “why freak out? Don’t you have something
better to do?” “Well, you’re a party pooper.”
Most news isn’t new. So people don’t do well learning new stuff. And when you’re trying to teach someone
something that’s new, you’re going to have a hard time getting it in. What people like to do is have what they
already know affirmed. They like to have what they already know affirmed, “You’re right. You’re smart.”

I’ll give you an example. Let’s say that I want to motivate you to take action right now in your life. I want to

you to feel that so that you will direct it into creating the most valuable information product you can. So what
I do is I decide to start teaching you about the molecular level of energy creation, how your cells actually create

And we drill down into the science, and I show you, actually, how the energy is released and how it works in
your body and how food and all the chemicals work. And I say, “Now that you understand how your body
creates energy, go do that to write an information product.” Most people would say, “Makes sense, right? Now
I know exactly how energy is created, and I want to have the energy to create my information product, but it’s

What if, on the other hand, I connected up two ideas that you already have in your mind? What if I connected
up where you’re at in your life and your business to the seasons? Okay, because you know where you’re at in
your life and your business, and you know what the seasons are, right, winter, spring, summer, fall?
And I say, “We’re all born in the springtime of our lives, when everything is new.” That’s when we’re born.
Everything seems fresh and inspiring. Everything is a mystery. We grow up through our springtime. That’s
when we do most of our learning in school. That’s when we take in our culture.
Then we move into the summer of our life. And at the beginning of summer, we start planting seeds for our
future success down the road. And then we start tending our garden. We start taking care of it. They say,
“Make hay while the sun shines.” Well, we only get a certain period of time that is summertime in our life.
And if we do a really good job in summertime, and we plant, and we tend, then, in midlife, we come into the
fall, and that’s harvest. That’s when we can really enjoy our life, and we can enjoy the fruits of our labor. If we
don’t do a good job planting in the summertime and tending our garden in the summertime, there will be no

“Okay, great. Go into the gym three times a week. So what I’d like to do is make a request of you. Can you

gym three times a week?” And the client says, “Yeah, I think I could commit to that.”
And then the coach says, “Great. I’d like to hold you accountable. Can I hold you accountable for doing those

not knowledge. My dad, when he was in college, he told me – or he told me later about his experience in
college. He said, “My experience was that the teachers there weren’t teachers, that they were tellers. They

program. I estimate that you are probably effectively communicating with 5 to 10 percent of your prospects and

And when you understand the various learning styles and meta programs and personality types, you can
communicate with all of them. And your results improve dramatically, both when you’re doing it in your
products, and when you’re doing it in your marketing.

There are two basic approaches when you’re trying to create success in life. One of them is the value creation
model and the other is the value extraction model. Most people are value extractors. They want to get
something. They want to extract value from a situation.
A lot of people that play the stock market, as an example. They don’t ask themselves the question, “Okay. I’ve
got $10,000.00. How can I create value for a business with this $10,000.00 so that we can both share in what
comes of it?” They say, “How do I take this $10,000.00 and use it to extract money from the stock market?”
That’s the essence of what’s going on.
Now, it doesn’t – it doesn’t make us bad if we’re value extractors, what have you. But I don’t think that it’s the

So as a general rule of thumb, beliefs stretch, but they don’t break. Beliefs stretch, but they don’t break.
Beliefs are – you might think of them as constructs. They’re artificial. They’re not real. They’re the result
of some mysterious process that we don’t fully understand yet. But it seems to be a thought or an idea
that we repeat enough times, and that we say yes to enough times that creates some kind of hard-wiring
or myelinization process in the brain, something like that. And that eventually we say, “Well I believe this
thing.”
And when most people say, I believe something, they’re saying, “That’s the way that it is.” So to them, when
they say I believe that this is the way that it is, what they’re really saying is, “That is the way it is.” That’s it.
Most people are not open to the idea of well, it’s just my belief, and there are probably other beliefs and other
ways of looking at it. And if I had other perspectives, I would probably think differently. That’s not the way

the average person thinks. To them, the belief is the way it is.
Beliefs are like drugs in that they are very addictive, highly addictive. And you’ll notice that when you try to
take someone’s belief away from them, or you try to break it, they immediately begin acting like an addict.
They get very nervous and sketchy, very uncomfortable, very irrational, very emotional, very defensive. When
you try to mess with someone’s belief too much, they begin acting like an addict.
So when I say, “Beliefs stretch. They don’t break.” If you try to break someone’s belief, not only will it not
work, but the person will in their mind marginalize you, discredit you and turn you into a wacko. If you want
a direct experience of this, talk to anyone that’s a real fundamentalist in their religion, and say, “God doesn’t

But that’s the way it works. Okay, humans are – you can think of it as literally, metaphorically, as literally

So you can’t tell someone that they’re wrong. Don’t tell people, “You are wrong,” or, “You’re bad,” or, “You
don’t get it,” unless you have massive rapport. My experience is that until you have either such massive
rapport or a relationship where the person psychologically gives you so much control or authority in the
situation, that if you say, “You’re wrong,” or, “You don’t get it,” it instantly breaks the relationship in a way
that’s hard to recover from.
There are only a few people in my life that I trust enough to say to me, “Hey Eben, there’s something that you
really don’t get. You’re really screwing up. You’re doing this backwards. Here’s the way it works,” that all my
defenses – I try to take a deep breath. Whatever is coming up I go, okay, let me get rid of that. I’m about to
learn something really good here, so bring it. Hit me with it.
When an average person comes up to me and says – I don’t mean average, but somebody I don’t really know
that well and says, “You know what? You’re really missing the boat here.” And they’re in their thing. I don’t
even – I really don’t even hear it. I kinda go into a mode of, “Oh, this person needs to be heard, and let me
just try to get into their reality so they can settle down a little bit.” It’s like a part of me just doesn’t even hear
the message. It’s hearing it for their sake and not for mine.
So what I like to do is instead of telling people they’re wrong or breaking their beliefs, I like to augment their
beliefs. I like to stretch their beliefs. I like to show them another perspective on their belief, show them an
unknown aspect. Add something that will help them make their belief even more valuable to them.
So how do you do this? Well, when you start with, “I’m not gonna tell anyone that they’re wrong anymore.

“A man convinced against his will is of the same opinion still.” So we’ll secretly harbor it. So when you let
go of all that, you say, “Okay, what am I gonna have to do in order to help a person augment or stretch their

Well, first you gotta get in line with them. You gotta get in step. You’ve gotta get into rapport. You gotta get

What I did is I set up a frame. That’s a frame that we’re all operating within, and it really helps to see 400 or

Contrast is another tool that can really facilitate intellectual understanding. Humans learn by association

we decide, “Is it similar, different? How?” And just by contrasting two things against each other, you can
really help someone understand how something works. You can also be very persuasive.
As an example, Dr. Robert Cialdini talks about in his book Influence, if you’re selling two items, sell the
highest priced item first because then the lower priced item will seem low-priced in comparison. Right? If
I wanna sell you these two items, and one of them’s $100, and one of them’s $1,000, and I say, “All right, buy
this $100 item.” I talk you into it. At the end I say, “Okay, great, now buy this $1,000 item.” Right, the $1,000
sounds like a higher price? The other way around, not so much.
Now, you have to play with this, by the way, because in selling information products, you might find that you
make more money by – if you’re selling online – by selling the lower priced item first because more people
will make the buying commitment and then up-sell to the higher priced item. But in an in-person selling
situation where they’re there, and you have control over the interaction, and you’re selling two things, selling
the higher priced item first, frames the lower priced item as being more of a bargain. And it’s an intellectual
understanding that you want them to have.

As a rule of thumb, predictability is boring. Predictability is boring. Surprises that create value are

So what do I mean by this? If you start writing, or you start teaching, and you say, “I’ll bet you are very sad
and disappointed right now that you can’t lose your weight.” Weight loss is a very complicated process.
Weight loss involves many steps that when integrated together results in the weight loss that you desire. Are

Jay Abraham says something great. He says, “Communicate with impact, or don’t communicate at all.”
“Communicate with impact, or don’t communicate at all.” If you find yourself ever putting filler into your
product or into your marketing, stop and don’t include that section because people become programmed very
quickly that what you’re saying isn’t valuable and isn’t important. And then they just pass you over, and they
tune you out. That’s what happens.
In the dating realm, one of the things that I say is you can’t bore a woman into feeling attracted to you,
although men try their damndest. So you can’t bore a woman into feeling attracted to you. You can’t bore
your customer into feeling motivated. You can’t bore a person into buying from you. Okay, they’re not gonna
hold out that long, especially in the modern era where there’s so much information and so many other things
– so many other stimuli that are trying to get their attention.
So surprises that create value are wonderful. So you have to speak their language enough so that you’re on
their page. You also have to introduce things that surprise and delight them, or at least keep their attention.
One of the things I’ve learned from Wyatt is if you can describe another person’s problem better than they
can, then they unconsciously assume that you know the answer. If you can describe their problem better
than they can, better than they can. Not as well as them, but even better. If you can really drill down and talk
about it in excruciating detail, that rivets them.
If I’m selling a product that helps a woman get and keep the attention of her own husband because she’s
insecure that he’s potentially losing interest, and I say, “It is common for men to become comfortable in their
relationships. This often leads to boredom with their spouse. Boredom with spouse often leads to a man
having a wondering eye, and this wondering eye occasionally leads to infidelity.” It’s like, duh. Duh.
On the other hand, if I say, “Have you ever caught your man looking at another woman? And I’m not talking

And then when he looks back at you, he looks a little bit startled. And he realizes that you know. And as you

of course, you were right. It’s an attractive woman. Your greatest fears have come true.”

Style. Write and communicate in the first person active. Present tense, right now. There’s a lot of people
like to talk in big words and nominalizations. Don’t say, “The man was observed approaching the woman
and initiating a conversation.” Say, “He walked over and said hi.” Or if you’re telling someone what to do, say,
“Walk over and say hi.”
“An approach of walking over and issuing a greeting is effective.” This is the way experts like to talk. This
weird passive voice. Don’t do that. Use short words. Direct, first person, active.
Keep your words and your sentences short, but your paragraphs – your words, your sentences and your
paragraphs short, but your copy long. Vary the length of your sentences and your paragraphs. Keep them
short, but vary the length. Every once in a while use a one word paragraph. Use a three-word paragraph, a
two-word paragraph, a one-word paragraph.
Put three words in a row separated by periods. Frank Kern is great at this. He’ll write about a marketing
situation and how it got really bad, and then he’ll write the words, all capital letters, “I. WAS. SCREWED.”
Awesome.
Pay attention to rhythm. Notice when you’re on a flow, you’re grooving, communicating. You got them in
there. And then stop. Punctuate. Pause. Dot, dot, dot. And then pick up the next thought.
Think of musical compositions. As you listen to music, realize that there’s a universal form to the way
movies, symphonies, conversations, relationships, they all go through a process. And if you can recognize
this pattern, starting with a bang, high energy, then getting into it and getting into the story, some surprises.
Conflicts come up. I don’t know what’s about to happen. Dilemmas. Themes that are repeating. These types
of things, all important to use in style.

Remember, your product is just another marketing piece. It’s a marketing piece to convince them to take

So name every concept that you create. If you’ve got a good idea, take the extra time to name it because let’s

Think about the difference between me saying, “I’d like to show you a way to get more leads for your business.

When you name something, you give it an identity. It becomes like a living entity. And when you describe it,

Name each of your products, of course, as well. Put even more time into naming your products because the

So there are four learning styles. And each of the four learning styles asks a primary question. And the four
questions are, why, what, how, and what if. Why, what, how, and what if. The why learning style wants to
know why they’re doing it. Why am I learning this? Why am I doing this thing? And they ask the question,
why? And what they wanna know is where are we going? What’s the outcome going to be? And they need
the goal, they need the picture, they need the outcome, in order to get motivated to go and take action.
This was a very profound learning – Wyatt taught me this, I don’t know, a couple of years ago now. And I

wondering if that’s why we have a little bit of a disconnect. And I explained this whole thing. Wyatt told me
that over 30 percent of humans are the why learning style. And as soon as I started explaining this, there were
some sighs of relief from the room, and people, on my team, were like, yeah, exactly. You don’t talk about
why we’re doing a certain thing, or why we’re learning this.
To me, being a different learning style, it’s apparent to me why we’re doing something. It’s obvious, like as

much. Why learning style needs to have the picture painted for them of the goal, again, so that they can get
motivated to do it. If you don’t explain why someone is learning something, in other words, the outcome that
they’re going to get, the result, the benefit that they’re going to enjoy, then they can’t learn. They experience a
learning block, and they sit there saying, why am I learning this? Why am I learning this? Why? Why, why,
why? And they’re confused and frustrated.
So with our team, I’ve now encouraged them, several times, if I start talking about something, and it’s not

oh, that’s right, I didn’t talk about what the outcome is. Why are we doing this? What are we doing this for?
What’s gonna happen if we do this? So the why learning style always wants to know why.

The what learning style asks the question, what? The what learning styles love all the theories, and the

intellectual, they want to know the theory, the concepts, how the whole thing works, show me the system.
The how learning style wants the procedure. They wanna know the step-by-step actions that they take.

So the how learning style needs action steps. A specific, a standard operating procedure of exactly what to

learn about it?
Okay, Joseph Campbell is a mythologist who is no longer alive. He was studying comparative religion in the
early part of last century, early-mid part of last century. And he traveled the world studying various cultures
and their religious traditions.
And as he was studying, he noticed that these traditions had stories that were bizarre, interesting, complex,
but that were very similar to each other. And these were cultures that had been separated for thousands of
years. All right, and they didn’t – there’s no way that the same story could have gotten to all of these different
cultures.
So he wrote a great book called The Hero with a Thousand Faces, and it’s all about this idea do what he called
the hero’s journey. And what he discovered is that there’s an archetypal story that the hero goes through in
the hero’s journey of life.
And his essential theory was that, because these stories are passed down from person to person, generation
to generation, it’s not that the same story got passed down but that the human psyche shaped every story to
match this universal story that we carry around inside of us.
And this archetypal story is the underlying framework behind, essentially, all great plays, movies, fiction
stories, etc. And it goes like this. And I’m going to simplify it a little bit just to make it easy to understand.
First thing that the hero experiences is the call to adventure. Something summons them to the great
adventure of their life. And what the hero does, when the hero hears the call to adventure, is they refuse the
call. They don’t go.
Then they’re called to adventure again, and this time, they go for it. They go on the path to adventure. Now,
on their journey, they run into obstacles. They face challenges, blocks. When this starts to happen, friends

136

GURU MasterClass Content Summit Disc 5
and helpers show up to assist them on their journey.
Oftentimes, a mentor and nemesis enter the picture. The hero gets to the goal of their journey, the brass ring.
And when they get there, they have to make a decision because when they finally do get their destiny, and
they’re about to make that choice, they realize that, if they make it, it’s going to mean something completely
different than they thought it would mean when they first left.
And then the hero makes the decision, one way or the other. And then they come back to the world with
what Joseph Campbell called the elixir. Now, they have the magical knowledge that they can give back to the
world. So that’s a summary of The Hero’s Journey.

Now, this is the basis for your product, the fears and frustrations and the wants and aspirations of your

Use stories and examples to make your point. Stories are very compelling. What they do is the get

And they’re very compelling, and they teach us things. They relate the learning that we’re having to things

When you add headings and subheadings, then you’re encapsulating the content for the reader. You’re

Anyway, in order to do that, I use a program called Snagit. You can get it free for 30 days at Snagit.com. It’s
an excellent program that just allows you to click a button and say, “I want this much of the screen.” You click
the copy button, paste it in your book, and boom, done.
Okay, sidebars, sidebars are really good. You know what a sidebar is? It’ll run across the right side of a page,
typically, and it’ll be a different story, totally different topic, like an example or something. These are really
good for case studies and related topics.
All right, so you can add a sidebar that is a story about somebody else who did whatever you’re talking about.
Put the whole thing in a sidebar. Format the sidebar. The sidebar needs margins. There needs to be margins
outside the side bar, inside the side bar. All of it takes up a little bit of space.
I also like to use pull quotes. And a pull quote, basically, is there’s text in the book, and I’m going to pull it
out, and I’m going to highlight it. And what that does is it allows you to emphasize important information.
You’ve seen bolding and italicizing on e-books, and that’s all fine. But sometimes, you want to make a big
point.
You can repeat important texts with a pull quote, and you can put in testimonials or very short cases studies
in there, as well, “Joe Smith made $1,200.00 with this technique in just one week,” whatever the idea is, or also
links to other resources too.
Now, something I saw in some books is – and it’s not something I use typically, but notes pages. So you can
just, either have a complete page full of blank lines – and only use this if this is appropriate for what you’re
doing.
For example, let’s say you have a log. You’re asking people to take a log. And here we go through these
exercises a lot. You can give people an exercise and then give them a big notes page, just a blank sheet with
lines, and let them fill in what they need to fill in. But you can actually add a notes page. It’s not something
that I typically do, but it’s something that you can do that, in your case, might be adding value just because
you’ve given people the space to do what they need to do, okay?
All right, now, I have a belief that the sale is not over once the credit card is charged. I think there’s more to
it than that. And as we said before, what’s the reason to write the e-book? What is it? Somebody has a real
want or a need, and you know something.
And I’ll tell you from the bottom of my heart, if somebody has a want or a need, and they manage to stumble
onto your website or something like that, if you don’t do everything you can to give them the solution that
they’ve been looking for, so that they can – we talked about knee pain a couple of times this weekend –
bounce their grandchild on their knee, which they couldn’t do before – call it a sin. Call it whatever you want.
You’ve got to do what you can to do it.
So your job is to solve your customer’s problem. You want to do everything you can do to solve your

You also want to educate your customer enough so that he’s ready to buy your next product. All right, so if

The last thing is you want to inspire your customer enough so that he wants to buy your next product. Now,

So your job in your introduction is to inspire them to read the book and then to inspire them to take action

I learned this phrase from Alex Mandossian, “Educate overly, and sell covertly.” What does that mean? In an

So you get to practice and blog every day if you can. Blog teaches you to write copy, teaches you to think,

And I’ve used it to ask questions. So I’ll twitter out to my list or I’ll e-mail and I’ll say, “Hey, what do you think

brain-dead simple for surveying. Does that make sense? It’s the most basic form of survey. You could also
use something like Survey Monkey, which is cheap and easy to use. You could also use something like the,
“Ask Database,” which is also cheap and easy to use. Go checkout surveymonkey.com and askdatabase.com,

Why do you want to survey? Because you want to get back what people think. You want to hear the words

Next, one-on-one coaching. There’s no faster way to get up-to-speed in learning your customers, learning

on inside the heads of people, to see what works and what doesn’t, to get a feel for it. After you’ve coached for
a while, you start anticipating. You start seeing where the challenges are, you see where the sticking points.
You learn what type of language works well.

day.” So on top of the one-minute meditation, I need to put the meditation reminder. This is what I’m gonna

Now there’s a few things that you might want to know about naming and about how naming works and how
to create a good name. There’s a part of the brain called the phonological loop, you may have heard me talk
about this. Each of our systems of perception have a buffer – the information comes in, bounces around in
that part of the brain, it kind of remembers it, temporarily, it’s the electrical memory, and then we decide
whether or not to decide – record it into chemical memory, kind of long-term. Most things don’t go in there
because they’re not valuable.
Well, the phonological loop is the auditory buffer. When information comes in auditorily, it bounces around
in there for three or four or five seconds. Now, what we want to do is, we want to get the name that you’re
creating to bounce around in there for a lot longer and there are ways to do that. There are certain types of
sounds that brains find attractive. One of those types of sounds is repetitive sounds, sounds that repeat.
Alliteration is very powerful. In other words, the first letter of two words is the same, as in “Double Your
Dating, the dd or Bed, Bath and Beyond, that’s alliteration.
And then there’s rhyme, where the end of the two words is the same, and you can mix these things up. I
mean, one of my favorite examples of a repetitive sound that’s a little bit weird and unusual is the Dixie
Chicks. The Dixie Chicks bounces around in your brain, why? I don’t know, there’s something to it, there’s a

You’ll notice that a lot of very successful companies an unusual amount of their names have a repetitive

The emotional states of creating content for newsletters and for any content, realize that there’s two different
parts of your brain. There’s a creative brain, where it’s literally flowing endlessly and it’s open and then there’s
the critical. Where I made the mistake, in the beginning, the most often, was that I combined the two. And
so I’d be writing something and I was creative and I’d be like, “Oh, no, no, no, that wasn’t good.” And I’d
delete it and I would go and cut it out and what I was doing, I was taking two states of my mind and I was
trying to weave them together and it made more confusion.
So have a process where you sit down and you just create a flow, no voice of criticism, just let it flow. Leave it,
go an hour, two hours, maybe even the next day, and then you put on your “editor’s hat.” You now have your
critical voice and you can look at it from that critical perspective. Be sure to separate the two process because
that’s really how your brain thinks. The three-point rifle sight is something that is very effective for writing
newsletters. If you have a shotgun – I’ve never fired a gun – but I’ve been told that if you fire a shotgun, it
goes everywhere, it sprays wide; and if you have a rifle, it [inaudible] right to where you’re aiming.
So really think about that when you’re writing a newsletter. The headline, the content and the call to action,
let it all line up. The headline is gonna be the first thing that your prospect sees in their inbox. And if they
click on that e-mail, it’s because they’re interested in that headline. If the headline and the content don’t
match up, they’re gonna be like, “What is this?” But if you have a headline that has the content and then the
call to action, it’s like a rifle shot, lining up.
So for an example, one of my highest converting newsletters is, “Five foods that melt fat.” I have a healthy
weight loss website for women and if a woman clicks on that headline, she’s interested in food, she wants to
know exactly what to eat, and so that’s what the content is all about.
If I were to talk to her about stress and weight gain or something like that, it would not match up, right? So

So here’s how it worked for me. I started with Double Your Dating and online marketing. Wrote an
e-book, started selling it, did not too bad with it, and then started building a list sending follow-up e-mail
newsletters. What I found is that by varying them, I seemed to keep people’s interest a little bit more because
they were changing.
So we actually used three types of newsletters. One is a tip, another one is what we call a Q & A, and the
other is what we call a mailbag. The tip is me writing for ten pages about an idea, about a particular topic that
I think will be relevant to a customer. A Q & A is like a tip, except that at the beginning, I’m answering one
question, and it’s usually a little bit longer.
So someone writes in and writes a letter and it asks a pretty interesting question. Sometimes there’ll be a
success story in there and then I’ll write a tip to answer that. And then the last one is a mailbag, which is just
me answering a bunch of random questions that came in, in the mail.
Now what this also allows me to do, is kind of communicate in a few different styles. So in the tip, it’s just me,
my voice, writing. I’m just talking to everyone and it’s my thing. In the Q & A, I’m answering someone. So as
I’m answering, I can keep calling back and referring to their questions and their comments, right? So I have
this little reference to go back to so there’s a little bit of conversational there and in the mailbag, some of the
questions are like one line.
They’ll say like, “Dude, why are you such a jackass?” And I love that kind of stuff because I can just go for
a while, right. And the meaning would be, if somebody says, “Why are you such a jackass?” I’ll say, okay,
so you could’ve written in and asked me, “How to meet Super Models,” and you asked, “Why are you such a
jackass?” or whatever. Or sometimes, somebody will write a really great question that’s several paragraphs
and I’ll include that and then I’ll answer it. And sometimes someone will write a several paragraph question
and I’ll answer it with one word. “Shoot yourself immediately” or whatever.
I did put, “shoot yourself immediately,” in one newsletter and I was crying when I wrote it because I was
laughing so hard at what a jackass the guy was. Although “shoot yourself immediately” was kind of at the end
of the rest of the thing and I didn’t say, “I don’t really mean it,” but I think you could have figured it out from
it. Then we went back and sent it out again, I took “shoot yourself immediately” out because I was too selfconscious about it.
But you can get pretty radical with people and they really appreciate it. I mean they really want the – they
want you to bring the truth to them. So those are the three formats that we use.
Now, what I found is that why it calls it edgeutainment is very powerful because we human primates, like

one of the most profound realizations you can have is to just ask yourself, “What do people pay for without
